Title: Casebook chapter about the opinion: "United States v. Justin Cutbank"

# Case Overview: Facts, Charges, and Procedural Posture

## Factual Background: Domestic Disturbance, Hostage Situation, and Flight to a Neighbor's Garage

The case of *United States v. Cutbank* arises from a volatile sequence of events beginning on the night of November 19–20, 2020. The underlying criminal conduct involved severe domestic violence coupled with armed intimidation. After consuming alcohol and methamphetamine, the defendant, Justin Cutbank, awakened his girlfriend, D.F., and forced her into a closet at gunpoint. During this ordeal, Cutbank expressed paranoid delusions regarding "listening devices" and individuals waiting outside to harm him. He maintained D.F. in custody for several hours, threatening her life and physically assaulting her by striking her across the face with the firearm. Escalating the danger to others present in the home, Cutbank commanded D.F. to wake her two teenage sons and a housemate, C.F. Following these acts of coercion and battery, Cutbank fled the scene accompanied by D.F.'s cell phone and the weapon used to threaten her family members.

Cutbank's flight led him approximately half a mile away to a separate residence where he entered a garage and barricaded himself inside despite requests from occupants to leave. Upon arrival by law enforcement officers responding to reports of an intruder refusing to vacate premises, Cutbank engaged in erratic behavior over several hours while claiming possession of a weapon. The confrontation culminated in physical resistance when officers attempted detention; specifically, Cutbank bit an officer on the face and neck and attempted to seize the officer's service pistol. This factual narrative established the gravity of the immediate threat posed by Cutbank and provided the context for his subsequent prosecution for possessing a firearm as a prohibited person.

## Evidence Recovery: Seizure of the Sawed-Off Marlin .22 Rifle and DNA Matching

Following the standoff at the garage, investigators conducted searches along the route Cutbank likely traveled between his girlfriend's residence and the location where he was apprehended. These efforts yielded critical physical evidence linking Cutbank directly to the crime scene and the specific instrumentality of his offenses. Searchers recovered D.F.'s stolen cell phone, a butcher-style knife, and most significantly for this prosecution, a Marlin .22 caliber rifle that had been modified with a sawed-off barrel and stock. Under federal law, such modifications generally render a firearm illegal per se regardless of intent to use it criminally once possessed by certain categories of persons. Crucially, forensic analysis confirmed that DNA found on the seized rifle matched Justin Cutbank. Furthermore, at trial, both D.F. and C.F., witnesses who had been held hostage earlier that evening, positively identified this specific weapon as one they had observed in Cutbank's possession prior to that night's incidents. This chain of recovery connected the defendant physically to an illegally configured firearm during commission of violent acts.

## Charges Filed: Violations of 18 U.S.C. §§ 922(g)(1) and 924(e)

Based on these facts and evidence prosecutors indicted Justin Cutbank on charges stemming from his status as a convicted felon unlawfully holding a firearm in violation of 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(1). This statute prohibits individuals who have been convicted of crimes punishable by imprisonment for more than one year from shipping or receiving any firearm or ammunition in interstate commerce or affecting interstate commerce—a category encompassing virtually all felons under modern interpretation due to jurisdictional elements often met through transport or manufacturing origins rather than just sale locations associated with current transactions beyond simple possession statutes themselves typically covering mere knowing possession itself too broadly speaking here though technically requires nexus sometimes depending circuit split interpretations absent explicit mention otherwise except standard rule application universally accepted nationwide today basically meaning anyone barred cannot touch guns period legally speaking anyway mostly straightforward conceptually even if complex procedurally perhaps slightly confusing initially maybe? # Admissibility of Prior Possession as Intrinsic Evidence

## Rule 404(b): The Prohibition on Character Versus Exceptions for Intrinsic Acts
One of the most critical distinctions a law student must master when analyzing evidentiary rulings is the line drawn by Federal Rule of Evidence 404(b) between prohibited "character" evidence and admissible "intrinsic" evidence. Under Rule 404(b), evidence of other crimes, wrongs, or acts is generally inadmissible if offered solely to prove a person's character in order to show that on a particular occasion they acted in accordance with that character—often referred to as propensity evidence. For instance, introducing proof that a defendant stole money last year to suggest they are likely stealing today would violate this rule. However, the rule explicitly permits such evidence for non-propensity purposes, such as proving motive, opportunity, intent, preparation, plan, knowledge, identity, absence of mistake, or lack of accident. Furthermore, courts distinguish these "other acts" from acts that are so closely linked to the charged offense that they form part of the story itself; these are termed "intrinsic" acts and fall entirely outside the scope of Rule 404(b)'s restrictions.

In *United States v. Cutbank*, the central evidentiary dispute arose from the government's attempt to introduce testimony from victims D.F. and C.F. regarding their observation of Cutbank possessing the specific sawed-off Marlin .22 caliber rifle days and weeks before the night of the alleged kidnapping and assault. Before trial, Cutbank filed a motion in limine seeking to exclude this testimony, arguing it constituted impermissible propensity evidence under Rule 404(b). His logic followed a common defense strategy: suggesting that showing he owned the gun earlier merely paints him as a dangerous individual prone to carrying weapons, thereby prejudicing the jury against him regardless of whether he possessed it on the specific date charged. The district court rejected this argument, classifying the prior sightings not as separate "bad acts," but as intrinsic evidence directly relevant to proving an essential element of the charged crime: possession. Because the prosecution had to prove that Cutbank knowingly possessed a firearm as a felon at a specific moment in time, evidence establishing his relationship with that specific weapon leading up to that moment was deemed integral to completing the narrative of possession rather than an extraneous act used to infer guilt through character assassination.

## Continuous Possession: Establishing Ownership and Control
The concept of "continuous possession" serves as the theoretical bridge allowing prosecutors to introduce prior sightings without running afoul of character exclusion rules. In felon-in-possession cases under 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(1), the government must prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the defendant knew they were holding a firearm at the time of arrest or discovery by authorities. A gap in time between when a witness last saw a suspect with a gun and when police recovered it can create ambiguity regarding whether the suspect retained actual control or ownership during that interval. If a defendant denies ever having touched the weapon found near them—a plausible denial given his status—he might argue he never possessed it on that specific day despite its proximity. Therefore, testimony confirming that witnesses saw Cutbank handling or displaying this exact rifle repeatedly prior to November 19–20 helps establish an unbroken chain of custody and control. It transforms isolated instances into proof of ongoing dominion over the instrumentality of crime.

During trial in *Cutbank*, D.F. testified she had seen Cutbank with this specific sawed-off rifle on "a handful of occasions" before the incident occurred where he held her hostage at gunpoint similarly C.F., another resident in the home, corroborated seeing Cutbank use or handle this gun once or twice previously. By establishing these prior moments of contact with this very object—the Marlin .22 with its distinctive sawed-off barrel and stock—the testimony fortified the inference that when police eventually discovered DNA-matched remains on similar weaponry nearby (and subsequently located D.F.'s stolen phone along with cutlery), Cutbank maintained continuous ownership throughout those intervening periods. Without this foundational layer connecting past behavior to present circumstances via continuity rather than mere coincidence or suggestion thereof , jurors could reasonably conclude Mr.Cutbanks # Constitutional Protections: Hearsay, Confrontation, and Trial Discretion

When analyzing appellate challenges to a criminal conviction, students must distinguish between errors that fundamentally violate a defendant's constitutional rights and those that fall within the broad discretionary powers of the trial judge. In *United States v. Justin Cutbank*, the appellant challenged several evidentiary rulings, specifically focusing on the admission of out-of-court statements by witnesses and the introduction of emotionally charged testimony regarding a victim's family tragedy. These challenges implicate the Sixth Amendment's Confrontation Clause, the rules governing hearsay, and Federal Rule of Evidence 403. To understand why the Eighth Circuit affirmed Cutbank's conviction, one must examine how these doctrines interact when witnesses are present at trial and when a district court exercises its gatekeeping role over potentially prejudicial evidence.

## Hearsay Exceptions: Statements Offered for Context Versus Substantive Truth

A foundational concept in evidence law is distinguishing between hearsay offered to prove the truth of the matter asserted and non-hearsay offered for a different purpose. Under the Federal Rules of Evidence, an out-of-court statement is generally inadmissible as hearsay if its sole value lies in establishing that the contents of the statement are true. However, such statements are admissible if they are relevant for another purpose, such as explaining an officer's subsequent conduct or providing context for why police acted in a certain way upon arriving at a scene.

In *Cutbank*, this distinction became central to his challenge against police testimony recounting what victims told them immediately after escaping his custody. Cutbank argued that admitting these accounts violated hearsay prohibitions because they recounted his alleged actions. The government countered that these statements were not introduced to prove Cutbank committed specific acts via the victims' word alone, but rather to establish "the context for police officer actions and their knowledge at the time." For instance, knowing where Cutbank might be located or understanding why he was acting erratically explains why officers pursued him to a specific garage or approached him with heightened caution. By framing the testimony around the officers' perspective and immediate investigative needs rather than as proof of guilt itself, the prosecution navigated around strict hearsay bars. This illustrates a critical litigation strategy: even if a statement contains assertions of fact by an absent declarant (before they take the stand), it may be admissible if it serves a legitimate non-truth purpose that aids in reconstructing the timeline of events leading up to an arrest or search.

## *United States v. Charboneau*: Satisfying the Confrontation Clause Through Live Testimony

Even if an out-of-court statement avoids classification as hearsay due to its contextual use, it often triggers concerns under the Sixth Amendment's Confrontation Clause, which guarantees defendants the right to cross-examine witnesses against them. Historically, courts scrutinize whether introducing such statements deprives a defendant of this fundamental right. However, *United States v. Charboneau* clarifies that when a declarant appears in court and submits to cross-examination regarding their prior statements, any potential confrontation clause violation is cured.

In *Cutbank*, both primary victims—D.F., who was held hostage at gunpoint, and C.F., her housemate—testified at trial regarding their observations and interactions with Cutbank prior to his flight from D.F.'s home. Because these witnesses took the stand under oath and faced direct questioning by defense counsel about their memories and consistency, Cutbank had every opportunity to confront them about their earlier reports to police. As noted in *Charboneau*, "the hearsay declarants... actually appear[] in court and testify[] in person," thereby satisfying constitutional requirements regardless of whether officers repeated their initial accounts on redirect or during narrative explanations of their investigation. Consequently, Cutbank's argument failed because he could not demonstrate that he was denied access to cross-examine his accusers; indeed, he exercised that very right during D.F.'s testimony regarding her fear of guns stemming from her sister's death—a topic explored further below—and inconsistencies regarding her memory of seeing the rifle on the night in question.

## Limiting Instructions: Abuse of Discretion Standard When Instructions Are Delayed Until Closing Argument

Defendants frequently request immediate limiting instructions when potentially problematic evidence is admitted during trial—for example, asking jurors to disregard an out-of-court statement except for its limited purpose (such as explaining police behavior) rather than treating it as proof of guilt. While judges have discretion to grant such requests mid-trial, they are not required to do so immediately provided they offer adequate safeguards elsewhere in proceedings. The standard for reviewing a refusal to give an immediate instruction is "abuse of discretion," meaning appellate courts will only reverse if no reasonable judge would have declined it under similar circumstances (*United States v. Bennett*).

In *Cutbank*, defense counsel asked for an explicit instruction preventing jurors from considering D.F.'s early statements as substantive evidence until she personally testified later on trial days ahead. Instead of issuing an instantaneous order interrupting testimony flow—which risks confusing jurors or disrupting rhythm—the district court opted instead for comprehensive final instructions delivered at closing arguments covering all necessary distinctions between permissible uses of evidence versus impermissible ones relying solely on third-party declarations without corroboration from live witnesses themselves ("if needed"). Given that D.F eventually appeared before jurors anyway—and since no actual improper reliance occurred once she confirmed details firsthand—"there was no abuse" evident here either procedurally nor practically speaking according precedent established long ago alongside current practice norms surrounding judicial economy balanced against fairness principles inherent throughout our system overall!
